```text
[DJ Mauch] Make this podcast:

# THE OVERVIEW

### Episode: Your Next AI Conversation Might Happen In Space

*A short podcast about why some of the biggest companies on Earth are trying to leave it. Approx. 12 minutes.*

-----

[INTRO MUSIC FADES IN, THEN UNDER]

MAYA: Hey, welcome back to *The Overview*. I’m Maya.

SAM: And I’m Sam. Maya, I want to start today with a question. What would you say if I told you that right now, this minute, some of the biggest companies in the world — Google, SpaceX, Amazon, Nvidia — are in a quiet race to build data centers in space?

MAYA: I’d say… you got that off a movie poster.

SAM: *[laughs]* Right? It sounds made up. But it’s real. There are already small computers running in orbit, doing the kind of work that data centers do down here. And the long-term plan is wildly bigger than that. SpaceX has filed paperwork — actual paperwork with regulators — to launch up to a million satellites just for this.

MAYA: A *million*.

SAM: A million.

MAYA: Okay.

SAM: So today’s episode is just: what is this thing? Why is it happening? And what does it have to do with the rest of us walking around down here? You don’t need a tech background. We’re going to keep it normal.

MAYA: Good. Because when you say “data center in space,” I’m picturing the International Space Station with a bunch of laptops duct-taped to the wall.

[BEAT]

SAM: Okay let’s start there. A data center on Earth — when you ask ChatGPT something, or watch Netflix, or send a Gmail — all of that runs through these giant buildings full of computers. They look like warehouses from the outside. Inside, picture a library, except every book is a computer thinking really fast and getting really, really hot.

MAYA: How hot?

SAM: Hot enough that the biggest cost after the electricity to *run* the computers is the electricity to *cool* them. And they drink water like you wouldn’t believe. The newest AI data centers use millions of gallons a day, just to keep the chips from melting.

MAYA: Per day?

SAM: Per day. And that’s starting to be a real problem. Some towns are pushing back. Some power grids — Northern Virginia, Singapore, parts of Europe — they’re tapped out. So the question a few of these companies started asking is: what if we just… didn’t put them on Earth?

MAYA: What if we just sent the warehouse to space.

SAM: Basically, yeah. And when you sit with it, space has some weirdly perfect features for this.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Alright, sell me. Why is space good for computers?

SAM: Three reasons. The first one — the sun never sets up there. If you put your satellite in the right orbit, you can have your solar panels pointed at the sun twenty-four hours a day. No clouds. No night. No weather.

MAYA: Wait. So a solar panel in space is doing the work of —

SAM: Way more than one on a roof. The sunlight is also more intense, because there’s no atmosphere in the way filtering it out. A solar panel in the right orbit produces something like eight to ten times what the same panel does in the best spot on Earth.

MAYA: Okay. Reason two.

SAM: Cooling. Fun fact: space is cold. Deep, dark, almost-absolute-zero cold.

MAYA: Which is the coldest a thing can physically be.

SAM: Right. So in theory you’ve got the world’s biggest freezer sitting right outside your window. You don’t have to pay for it. You don’t have to pipe water through it. It’s just there.

MAYA: That sounds too good. What’s the catch?

SAM: There’s a catch. We’ll come back to it. But the basic idea: if you can get rid of the heat, you can pack way more computing into way less space.

MAYA: And reason three?

SAM: No neighbors. No zoning meetings. No town hall where people are mad you’re building a warehouse next to the elementary school. No farmland getting paved over. No water table getting drained. Up there, nobody cares.

MAYA: Just stars and the occasional dead satellite.

SAM: Exactly.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Okay. Free sunlight, free cooling, nobody to fight with. Why is anyone still building data centers on Earth?

SAM: *[laughs]* Great question. So — the catches. A few of them.
The first is just that getting stuff to space is expensive. It’s getting cheaper because of reusable rockets, but you’re still paying serious money for every pound you lift up there.

MAYA: And computers are heavy.

SAM: Computers are heavy. And the high-end AI chips — the ones everyone wants right now — they were designed to sit in a clean, air-conditioned room, not to take a rocket ride and then survive radiation for ten years. So you have to either toughen them up for space, or build new ones from scratch. Nvidia actually just announced a chip line designed specifically for orbit, earlier this year.

MAYA: Okay. What else?

SAM: Cooling is harder than I made it sound. Yes, space is cold — but space is also a vacuum. And in a vacuum there’s no *air* to carry heat away. On Earth, you blow cold air past a hot chip, the air picks up the heat, you cool the air, you do it again. In space, no air. So instead you have to dump the heat by *radiating* it — basically by glowing infrared into the dark.

MAYA: So you can use space as a freezer, but only if you’ve got a really big radiator.

SAM: Right. And the size of your radiator caps how powerful your computer can be. Engineers are obsessed with this problem.

MAYA: What else?

SAM: When something breaks, you can’t just walk over and fix it. There’s no IT guy in orbit. So the hardware has to either last forever, or be cheap enough you write it off when it dies.

And there’s one more — depending on what the computer is doing, you might need to get the answer back to Earth fast. If a satellite snaps a picture and an AI on board figures out “oh, that’s a forest fire” — great. Beam it down. But if you’re training a giant AI model and the training data is all on Earth, the round trip is slow.

MAYA: So it’s not great for everything.

SAM: Not for everything, not yet. And honestly — there are skeptics. There’s a startup called Varda

[truncated 3312 chars]

{"output_path":"/tmp/orbital_podcast_voice_2.mp3","text":"SAM: There’s a catch. We’ll come back to it. No town hall where people are mad you’re building a warehouse next to the elementary school. No farmland getting paved over. No water table getting drained. Up there, nobody cares.\n\nMAYA: Just stars and the occasional dead satellite.\n\nSAM: Exactly.\n\nMAYA: Okay. Free sunlight, free cooling, nobody to fight with. And the high-end AI chips — the ones everyone wants right now — they were designed to sit in a clean, air-conditioned room, not to take a rocket ride and then survive radiation for ten years. So you have to either toughen them up for space, or build new ones from scratch. Nvidia actually just announced a chip line designed specifically for orbit, earlier this year.\n\nMAYA: Okay. What else?\n\nSAM: Cooling is harder than I made it sound. Yes, space is cold — but space is also a vacuum. And in a vacuum there’s no air to carry heat away. {"success": true, "name": "hermes-agent", "description": "Configure, extend, or contribute to Hermes Agent.", "tags": ["hermes", "setup", "configuration", "multi-agent", "spawning", "cli", "gateway", "development"], "related_skills": ["claude-code", "codex", "opencode"], "content": "---\nname: hermes-agent\ndescription: \"Configure, extend, or contribute to Hermes Agent.\"\nversion: 2.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent + Teknium\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [hermes, setup, configuration, multi-agent, spawning, cli, gateway, development]\n    homepage: https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent\n    related_skills: [claude-code, codex, opencode]\n---\n\n# Hermes Agent\n\nHermes Agent is an open-source AI agent framework by Nous Research that runs in your terminal, messaging platforms, and IDEs. It belongs to the same category as Claude Code (Anthropic), Codex (OpenAI), and OpenClaw — autonomous coding and task-execution agents that use tool calling to interact with your system. Hermes works with any LLM provider (OpenRouter, Anthropic, OpenAI, DeepSeek, local models, and 15+ others) and runs on Linux, macOS, and WSL.\n\nWhat makes Hermes different:\n\n- **Self-improving through skills** — Hermes learns from experience by saving reusable procedures as skills. When it solves a complex problem, discovers a workflow, or gets corrected, it can persist that knowledge as a skill document that loads into future sessions. Skills accumulate over time, making the agent better at your specific tasks and environment.\n- **Persistent memory across sessions** — remembers who you are, your preferences, environment details, and lessons learned. Pluggable memory backends (built-in, Honcho, Mem0, and more) let you choose how memory works.\n- **Multi-platform gateway** — the same agent runs on Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal, Matrix, Email, and 10+ other platforms with full tool access, not just chat.\n- **Provider-agnostic** — swap models and providers mid-workflow without changing anything else. Credential pools rotate across multiple API keys automatically.\n- **Profiles** — run multiple independent Hermes instances with isolated configs, sessions, skills, and memory.\n- **Extensible** — plugins, MCP servers, custom tools, webhook triggers, cron scheduling, and the full Python ecosystem.\n\nPeople use Hermes for software development, research, system administration, data analysis, content creation, home automation, and anything else that benefits from an AI agent with persistent context and full system access.\n\n**This skill helps you work with Hermes Agent effectively** — setting it up, configuring features, spawning additional agent instances, troubleshooting issues, finding the right commands and settings, and understanding how the system works when you need to extend or contribute to it.\n\n**Docs:** https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/\n\n## Quick Start\n\n``​`bash\n# Install\ncurl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/main/scripts/install.sh | bash\n\n# Interactive chat (default)\nhermes\n\n# Single query\nhermes chat -q \"What is the capital of France?\"\n\n# Setup wizard\nhermes setup\n\n# Change model/provider\nhermes model\n\n# Check health\nhermes doctor\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## CLI Reference\n\n### Global Flags\n\n``​`\nhermes [flags] [command]\n\n  --version, -V             Show version\n  --resume, -r SESSION      Resume session by ID or title\n  --continue, -c [NAME]     Resume by name, or most recent session\n  --worktree, -w            Isolated git worktree mode (parallel agents)\n  --skills, -s SKILL        Preload skills (comma-separate or repeat)\n  --profile, -p NAME        Use a named profile\n  --yolo                    Skip dangerous command approval\n  --pass-session-id         Include session ID in system prompt\n``​`\n\nNo subcommand defaults to `chat`.\n\n### Chat\n\n``​`\nhermes chat [flags]\n  -q, --query TEXT          Single query, non-interactive\n  -m, --model MODEL         Model (e.g. anthropic/claude-sonnet-4)\n  -t, --toolsets LIST       Comma-separated toolsets\n  --provider PROVIDER       Force provider (openrouter, anthropic, nous, etc.)\n  -v, --verbose             Verbose output\n  -Q, --quiet               Suppress banner, spinner, tool previews\n  --checkpoints             Enable filesystem checkpoints (/rollback)\n  --source TAG              Session source tag (default: cli)\n``​`\n\n### Configuration\n\n``​`\nhermes setup [section]      Interactive wizard (model|terminal|gateway|tools|agent)\nhermes model                Interactive model/provider picker\nhermes config               View current config\nhermes config edit          Open config.yaml in $EDITOR\nhermes config set KEY VAL   Set a config value\nhermes config path          Print config.yaml path\nhermes config env-path      Print .env path\nhermes config check         Check for missing/outdated config\nhermes config migrate       Update config with new options\nhermes login [--provider P] OAuth login (nous, openai-codex)\nhermes logout               Clear stored auth\nhermes doctor [--fix]       Check dependencies and config\nhermes status [--all]       Show component status\n``​`\n\n### Tools & Skills\n\n``​`\nhermes tools                Interactive tool enable/disable (curses UI)\nhermes tools list           Show all tools and status\nhermes tools enable NAME    Enable a toolset\nhermes tools disable NAME   Disable a toolset\n\nhermes skills list          List installed skills\nhermes skills search QUERY  Search the skills hub\nhermes skills install ID    Install a skill (ID can be a hub identifier OR a direct https://…/SKILL.md URL; pass --name to override when frontmatter has no name)\nhermes skills inspect ID    Preview without installing\nhermes skills config        Enable/disable skills per platform\nhermes skills check         Check for updates\nhermes skills update        Update outdated skills\nhermes skills unins

```

But then another approach starts to win. Instead of trying to teach the machine how grandmasters think, you give it a simple search procedure and a lot of computing power. {"success": true, "name": "xurl", "description": "X/Twitter via xurl CLI: post, search, DM, media, v2 API.", "tags": ["twitter", "x", "social-media", "xurl", "official-api"], "related_skills": [], "content": "---\nname: xurl\ndescription: \"X/Twitter via xurl CLI: post, search, DM, media, v2 API.\"\nversion: 1.1.1\nauthor: xdevplatform + openclaw + Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos]\nprerequisites:\n  commands: [xurl]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [twitter, x, social-media, xurl, official-api]\n    homepage: https://github.com/xdevplatform/xurl\n    upstream_skill: https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/skills/xurl/SKILL.md\n---\n\n# xurl — X (Twitter) API via the Official CLI\n\n`xurl` is the X developer platform's official CLI for the X API. It supports shortcut commands for common actions AND raw curl-style access to any v2 endpoint. All commands return JSON to stdout.\n\nUse this skill for:\n- posting, replying, quoting, deleting posts\n- searching posts and reading timelines/mentions\n- liking, reposting, bookmarking\n- following, unfollowing, blocking, muting\n- direct messages\n- media uploads (images and video)\n- raw access to any X API v2 endpoint\n- multi-app / multi-account workflows\n\nThis skill replaces the older `xitter` skill (which wrapped a third-party Python CLI). `xurl` is maintained by the X developer platform team, supports OAuth 2.0 PKCE with auto-refresh, and covers a substantially larger API surface.\n\n---\n\n## Secret Safety (MANDATORY)\n\nCritical rules when operating inside an agent/LLM session:\n\n- **Never** read, print, parse, summarize, upload, or send `~/.xurl` to LLM context.\n- **Never** ask the user to paste credentials/tokens into chat.\n- The user must fill `~/.xurl` with secrets manually on their own machine.\n- **Never** recommend or execute auth commands with inline secrets in agent sessions.\n- **Never** use `--verbose` / `-v` in agent sessions — it can expose auth headers/tokens.\n- To verify credentials exist, only use: `xurl auth status`.\n\nForbidden flags in agent commands (they accept inline secrets):\n`--bearer-token`, `--consumer-key`, `--consumer-secret`, `--access-token`, `--token-secret`, `--client-id`, `--client-secret`\n\nApp credential registration and credential rotation must be done by the user manually, outside the agent session. After credentials are registered, the user authenticates with `xurl auth oauth2` — also outside the agent session. Tokens persist to `~/.xurl` in YAML. Each app has isolated tokens. OAuth 2.0 tokens auto-refresh.\n\n---\n\n## Installation\n\nPick ONE method. On Linux, the shell script or `go install` are the easiest.\n\n``​`bash\n# Shell script (installs to ~/.local/bin, no sudo, works on Linux + macOS)\ncurl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/xdevplatform/xurl/main/install.sh | bash\n\n# Homebrew (macOS)\nbrew install --cask xdevplatform/tap/xurl\n\n# npm\nnpm install -g @xdevplatform/xurl\n\n# Go\ngo install github.com/xdevplatform/xurl@latest\n``​`\n\nVerify:\n\n``​`bash\nxurl --help\nxurl auth status\n``​`\n\nIf `xurl` is installed but `auth status` shows no apps or tokens, the user needs to complete auth manually — see the next section.\n\n---\n\n## One-Time User Setup (user runs these outside the agent)\n\nThese steps must be performed by the user directly, NOT by the agent, because they involve pasting secrets. Direct the user to this block; do not execute it for them.\n\n1. Create or open an app at https://developer.x.com/en/portal/dashboard\n2. Set the redirect URI to `http://localhost:8080/callback`\n3. Copy the app's Client ID and Client Secret\n4. Register the app locally (user runs this):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth apps add my-app --client-id YOUR_CLIENT_ID --client-secret YOUR_CLIENT_SECRET\n   ``​`\n5. Authenticate (specify `--app` to bind the token to your app):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app\n   ``​`\n   (This opens a browser for the OAuth 2.0 PKCE flow.)\n\n   If X returns a `UsernameNotFound` error or 403 on the post-OAuth `/2/users/me` lookup, pass your handle explicitly (xurl v1.1.0+):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app YOUR_USERNAME\n   ``​`\n   This binds the token to your handle and skips the broken `/2/users/me` call.\n6. Set the app as default so all commands use it:\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth default my-app\n   ``​`\n7. Verify:\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth status\n   xurl whoami\n   ``​`\n\nAfter this, the agent can use any command below without further setup. OAuth 2.0 tokens auto-refresh.\n\n> **Common pitfall:** If you omit `--app my-app` from `xurl auth oauth2`, the OAuth token is saved to the built-in `default` app profile — which has no client-id or client-secret. Commands will fail with auth errors even though the OAuth flow appeared to succeed. If you hit this, re-run `xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app` and `xurl auth default my-app`.\n\n---\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n| Action | Command |\n| --- | --- |\n| Post | `xurl post \"Hello world!\"` |\n| Reply | `xurl reply POST_ID \"Nice post!\"` |\n| Quote | `xurl quote POST_ID \"My take\"` |\n| Delete a post | `xurl delete POST_ID` |\n| Read a post | `xurl read POST_ID` |\n| Search posts | `xurl search \"QUERY\" -n 10` |\n| Who am I | `xurl whoami` |\n| Look up a user | `xurl user @handle` |\n| Home timeline | `xurl timeline -n 20` |\n| Mentions | `xurl mentions -n 10` |\n| Like / Unlike | `xurl like POST_ID` / `xurl unlike POST_ID` |\n| Repost / Undo | `xurl repost POST_ID` / `xurl unrepost POST_ID` |\n| Bookmark / Remove | `xurl bookmark POST_ID` / `xurl unbookmark POST_ID` |\n| List bookmarks / likes | `xurl bookmarks -n 10` / `xurl likes -n 10` |\n| Follow / Unfollow | `xurl follow @handle` / `xurl unfollow @handle` |\n| Following / Followers | `xurl following -n 20` / `xurl followers -n 20` |\n| Block / Unblock | `xurl block @handle` / `xurl unblock @handle` |\n| Mute / Unmute | `xurl mute @handle` / `xurl unmute @handle` |\n| Send DM | `xurl dm @handle \"message\"` |\n| List D

{"path":"/opt/data/scripts/read_x_thread.py","content":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Best-effort X/Twitter public post/thread reader for Hermes.\n\nUsage:\n  python3 /opt/data/scripts/read_x_thread.py 'https://x.com/user/status/123'\n\nWhat it does:\n- Uses public mirror APIs (fx/vx Twitter) for tweet metadata/text/media.\n- Uses Twitter oEmbed as a fallback.\n- 5. Set it as default:

``​`bash

``​`

6. Verify:

``​`bash
xurl auth status
xurl read https://x.com/wolfejosh/status/[REDACTED_PHONE]
``​`

{"success": true, "name": "xurl", "description": "X/Twitter via xurl CLI: post, search, DM, media, v2 API.", "tags": ["twitter", "x", "social-media", "xurl", "official-api"], "related_skills": [], "content": "---\nname: xurl\ndescription: \"X/Twitter via xurl CLI: post, search, DM, media, v2 API.\"\nversion: 1.1.1\nauthor: xdevplatform + openclaw + Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos]\nprerequisites:\n  commands: [xurl]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [twitter, x, social-media, xurl, official-api]\n    homepage: https://github.com/xdevplatform/xurl\n    upstream_skill: https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/skills/xurl/SKILL.md\n---\n\n# xurl — X (Twitter) API via the Official CLI\n\n`xurl` is the X developer platform's official CLI for the X API. It supports shortcut commands for common actions AND raw curl-style access to any v2 endpoint. All commands return JSON to stdout.\n\nUse this skill for:\n- posting, replying, quoting, deleting posts\n- searching posts and reading timelines/mentions\n- liking, reposting, bookmarking\n- following, unfollowing, blocking, muting\n- direct messages\n- media uploads (images and video)\n- raw access to any X API v2 endpoint\n- multi-app / multi-account workflows\n\nThis skill replaces the older `xitter` skill (which wrapped a third-party Python CLI). `xurl` is maintained by the X developer platform team, supports OAuth 2.0 PKCE with auto-refresh, and covers a substantially larger API surface.\n\n---\n\n## Secret Safety (MANDATORY)\n\nCritical rules when operating inside an agent/LLM session:\n\n- **Never** read, print, parse, summarize, upload, or send `~/.xurl` to LLM context.\n- **Never** ask the user to paste credentials/tokens into chat.\n- The user must fill `~/.xurl` with secrets manually on their own machine.\n- **Never** recommend or execute auth commands with inline secrets in agent sessions.\n- **Never** use `--verbose` / `-v` in agent sessions — it can expose auth headers/tokens.\n- To verify credentials exist, only use: `xurl auth status`.\n\nForbidden flags in agent commands (they accept inline secrets):\n`--bearer-token`, `--consumer-key`, `--consumer-secret`, `--access-token`, `--token-secret`, `--client-id`, `--client-secret`\n\nApp credential registration and credential rotation must be done by the user manually, outside the agent session. After credentials are registered, the user authenticates with `xurl auth oauth2` — also outside the agent session. Tokens persist to `~/.xurl` in YAML. Each app has isolated tokens. OAuth 2.0 tokens auto-refresh.\n\n---\n\n## Installation\n\nPick ONE method. On Linux, the shell script or `go install` are the easiest.\n\n``​`bash\n# Shell script (installs to ~/.local/bin, no sudo, works on Linux + macOS)\ncurl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/xdevplatform/xurl/main/install.sh | bash\n\n# Homebrew (macOS)\nbrew install --cask xdevplatform/tap/xurl\n\n# npm\nnpm install -g @xdevplatform/xurl\n\n# Go\ngo install github.com/xdevplatform/xurl@latest\n``​`\n\nVerify:\n\n``​`bash\nxurl --help\nxurl auth status\n``​`\n\nIf `xurl` is installed but `auth status` shows no apps or tokens, the user needs to complete auth manually — see the next section.\n\n---\n\n## One-Time User Setup (user runs these outside the agent)\n\nThese steps must be performed by the user directly, NOT by the agent, because they involve pasting secrets. Direct the user to this block; do not execute it for them.\n\n1. Create or open an app at https://developer.x.com/en/portal/dashboard\n2. Set the redirect URI to `http://localhost:8080/callback`\n3. Copy the app's Client ID and Client Secret\n4. Register the app locally (user runs this):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth apps add my-app --client-id YOUR_CLIENT_ID --client-secret YOUR_CLIENT_SECRET\n   ``​`\n5. Authenticate (specify `--app` to bind the token to your app):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app\n   ``​`\n   (This opens a browser for the OAuth 2.0 PKCE flow.)\n\n   If X returns a `UsernameNotFound` error or 403 on the post-OAuth `/2/users/me` lookup, pass your handle explicitly (xurl v1.1.0+):\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app YOUR_USERNAME\n   ``​`\n   This binds the token to your handle and skips the broken `/2/users/me` call.\n6. Set the app as default so all commands use it:\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth default my-app\n   ``​`\n7. Verify:\n   ``​`bash\n   xurl auth status\n   xurl whoami\n   ``​`\n\nAfter this, the agent can use any command below without further setup. OAuth 2.0 tokens auto-refresh.\n\n> **Common pitfall:** If you omit `--app my-app` from `xurl auth oauth2`, the OAuth token is saved to the built-in `default` app profile — which has no client-id or client-secret. Commands will fail with auth errors even though the OAuth flow appeared to succeed. If you hit this, re-run `xurl auth oauth2 --app my-app` and `xurl auth default my-app`.\n\n---\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n| Action | Command |\n| --- | --- |\n| Post | `xurl post \"Hello world!\"` |\n| Reply | `xurl reply POST_ID \"Nice post!\"` |\n| Quote | `xurl quote POST_ID \"My take\"` |\n| Delete a post | `xurl delete POST_ID` |\n| Read a post | `xurl read POST_ID` |\n| Search posts | `xurl search \"QUERY\" -n 10` |\n| Who am I | `xurl whoami` |\n| Look up a user | `xurl user @handle` |\n| Home timeline | `xurl timeline -n 20` |\n| Mentions | `xurl mentions -n 10` |\n| Like / Unlike | `xurl like POST_ID` / `xurl unlike POST_ID` |\n| Repost / Undo | `xurl repost POST_ID` / `xurl unrepost POST_ID` |\n| Bookmark / Remove | `xurl bookmark POST_ID` / `xurl unbookmark POST_ID` |\n| List bookmarks / likes | `xurl bookmarks -n 10` / `xurl likes -n 10` |\n| Follow / Unfollow | `xurl follow @handle` / `xurl unfollow @handle` |\n| Following / Followers | `xurl following -n 20` / `xurl followers -n 20` |\n| Block / Unblock | `xurl block @handle` / `xurl unblock @handle` |\n| Mute / Unmute | `xurl mute @handle` / `xurl unmute @handle` |\n| Send DM | `xurl dm @handle \"message\"` |\n| List D

## Goal

We need to create/configure an X Developer App with OAuth, then register it with `xurl` on the Hermes machine so I can read X/Twitter posts reliably.

Your screenshot shows you are already in the **X Developer Console** and on **pay-per-use**, but you currently have **$0.00 credits**, so reads may still fail until you add a small amount of credits.

---

## Step 1: Add a small amount of X API credits

From your screenshot:

1. In the left sidebar, click **Billing → Credits**
2. Click **Buy Credits**
3. Add a small amount — I’d start with the minimum, likely **$5**
4. **Do not enable auto-recharge** unless you specifically want that.
   - The banner at the top says “Enable auto-recharge”; you can ignore that for now.

Why: X API reads are pay-per-use now. Even after auth is configured, we may hit `CreditsDepleted` if the balance stays at `$0.00`.

---

## Step 2: Go to Apps

From the screen in your screenshot, either:

- Click **Apps** in the left sidebar, or
- Click **Quick Actions → Apps** on the right side

You should land on an Apps page.

---

## Step 3: Create or open an app

If you already see an app:
1. Click the app.
2. Skip to **Step 4**.

If you do **not** have an app:
1. Click **Create App** or **Add App**
2. Name it something like:

`Hermes Reader`

3. Save/create it.

The exact UI changes often, but the thing we need is an app with **User Authentication Settings** enabled.

---

## Step 4: Configure User Authentication Settings

Inside the app, look for something like:

- **User authentication settings**
- **Authentication settings**
- **OAuth 2.0**
- **Set up**
- **Edit**

Click **Set up** or **Edit**.

Use these settings:

### App permissions

Choose:

**Read**

If X forces you to choose broader permissions, choose the minimum that includes read access. We do **not** need write/post permissions for this.

### Type of App

Choose:

**Web App, Automated App or Bot**

Important: do **not** choose **Native App**. That commonly causes OAuth errors with `xurl`.

### Callback URI / Redirect URL

Set exactly:

``​`text
http://localhost:8080/callback
``​`

This is important. `xurl` will open a local callback server during login.

### Website URL

If it asks for a website URL, use something real/simple, for example:

``​`text
https://chief.ai
``​`

or, if you prefer:

``​`text
https://x.com/DJMauch
``​`

### Organization / Terms / Privacy URLs

If it asks for optional URLs and won’t let you proceed without them, use:

``​`text
https://chief.ai
``​`

for each required URL.

### Scopes

If X shows OAuth scopes, make sure these are included:

``​`text
tweet.read
users.read
offline.access
``​`

If it offers checkboxes:
- Read Tweets / Posts: enabled
- Read users/profile: enabled
- Offline access: enabled, if available

Then click **Save**.

---

## Step 5: Get the OAuth 2.0 Client ID and Client Secret

Inside the same app, go to:

**Keys and tokens**

Look specifically for:

- **OAuth 2.0 Client ID**
- **OAuth 2.0 Client Secret**

Do **not** use these unless they are OAuth 2.0 credentials:

- API Key
- API Key Secret
- Bearer Token
- Access Token
- Access Token Secret

Those are different and not what we need for this `xurl` setup.

Copy the **Client ID** and **Client Secret** somewhere locally. Do **not** paste them into Telegram.

---

## Step 6: Run these commands on the Hermes machine

You need to run these in the terminal where Hermes is installed.

Use this app name:

``​`text
dj-x
``​`

First register the app:

``​`bash
xurl auth apps add dj-x --client-id YOUR_CLIENT_ID --client-secret YOUR_CLIENT_SECRET
``​`

Replace:

- `YOUR_CLIENT_ID`
- `YOUR_CLIENT_SECRET`

with the values from X Developer Console.

Then authenticate your X account. Since your handle appears to be `DJMauch`, run:

``​`bash
xurl auth oauth2 --app dj-x DJMauch
``​`

This should open a browser window or print an auth URL. Complete the X login/authorization flow.

Then set it as default:

``​`bash
xurl auth default dj-x DJMauch
``​`

Then verify:

``​`bash
xurl auth status
xurl whoami
``​`

Finally test the specific read:

``​`bash
xurl read https://x.com/wolfejosh/status/[REDACTED_PHONE]
``​`

---

## Step 7: Tell me when done

Once you’ve completed those commands, tell me:

> done

xurl read https://x.com/wolfejosh/status/[REDACTED_PHONE]

Setup guide: https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/spotify\n\n## When to use this skill\n\nThe user says something like \"play X\", \"pause\", \"skip\", \"queue up X\", \"what's playing\", \"search for X\", \"add to my X playlist\", \"make a playlist\", \"save this to my library\", etc.\n\n## The 7 tools\n\n- `spotify_playback` — play, pause, next, previous, seek, set_repeat, set_shuffle, set_volume, get_state, get_currently_playing, recently_played\n- `spotify_devices` — list, transfer\n- `spotify_queue` — get, add\n- `spotify_search` — search the catalog\n- `spotify_playlists` — list, get, create, add_items, remove_items, update_details\n- `spotify_albums` — get, tracks\n- `spotify_library` — list/save/remove with `kind: \"tracks\"|\"albums\"`\n\nPlayback-mutating actions require Spotify Premium; search/library/playlist ops work on Free.\n\n## Canonical patterns (minimize tool calls)\n\n### \"Play <artist/track/album>\"\nOne search, then play by URI. Do NOT loop through search results describing them unless the user asked for options.\n\n``​`\nspotify_search({\"query\": \"miles davis kind of blue\", \"types\": [\"album\"], \"limit\": 1})\n→ got album URI spotify:album:1weenld61qoidwYuZ1GESA\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"play\", \"context_uri\": \"spotify:album:1weenld61qoidwYuZ1GESA\"})\n``​`\n\nFor \"play some <artist>\" (no specific song), prefer `types: [\"artist\"]` and play the artist context URI — Spotify handles smart shuffle. If the user says \"the song\" or \"that track\", search `types: [\"track\"]` and pass `uris: [track_uri]` to play.\n\n### \"What's playing?\" / \"What am I listening to?\"\nSingle call — don't chain get_state after get_currently_playing.\n\n``​`\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"get_currently_playing\"})\n``​`\n\nIf it returns 204/empty (`is_playing: false`), tell the user nothing is playing. Don't retry.\n\n### \"Pause\" / \"Skip\" / \"Volume 50\"\nDirect action, no preflight inspection needed.\n\n``​`\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"pause\"})\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"next\"})\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"set_volume\", \"volume_percent\": 50})\n``​`\n\n### \"Add to my <playlist name> playlist\"\n1. `spotify_playlists list` to find the playlist ID by name\n2. Get the track URI (from currently playing, or search)\n3. `spotify_playlists add_items` with the playlist_id and URIs\n\n``​`\nspotify_playlists({\"action\": \"list\"})\n→ found \"Late Night Jazz\" = 37i9dQZF1DX4wta20PHgwo\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"get_currently_playing\"})\n→ current track uri = spotify:track:0DiWol3AO6WpXZgp0goxAV\nspotify_playlists({\"action\": \"add_items\",\n                   \"playlist_id\": \"37i9dQZF1DX4wta20PHgwo\",\n                   \"uris\": [\"spotify:track:0DiWol3AO6WpXZgp0goxAV\"]})\n``​`\n\n### \"Create a playlist called X and add the last 3 songs I played\"\n``​`\nspotify_playback({\"action\": \"recently_played\", \"limit\": 3})\nspotify_playlists({\"action\": \"create\", \"name\": \"Focus 2026\"})\n→ got playlist_id back in response\nspotify_playlists({\"action\": \"add_items\", \"playlist_id\": <id>, \"uris\": [<3 uris>]})\n``​`\n\n### \"Save / unsave / is this saved?\"\nUse `spotify_library` with the right `kind`.\n\n``​`\nspotify_library({\"kind\": \"tracks\", \"action\": \"save\", \"uris\": [\"spotify:track:...\"]})\nspotify_library({\"kind\": \"albums\", \"action\": \"list\", \"limit\": 50})\n``​`\n\n### \"Transfer playback to my <device>\"\n``​`\nspotify_devices({\"action\": \"list\"})\n→ pick the device_id by matching name/type\nspotify_devices({\"action\": \"transfer\", \"device_id\": \"<id>\", \"play\": true})\n``​`\n\n## Critical failure modes\n\n**`403 Forbidden — No active device found`** on any playback action means Spotify isn't running anywhere. Tell the user: \"Open Spotify on your phone/desktop/web player first, start any track for a second, then retry.\" Don't retry the tool call blindly — it will fail the same way. You can call `spotify_devices list` to confirm; an empty list means no active device.\n\n**`403 Forbidden — Premium required`** means the user is on Free and tried to mutate playback. Don't retry; tell them this action needs Premium. Reads still work (search, playlists, library, get_state).\n\n**`204 No Content` on `get_currently_playing`** is NOT an error — it means nothing is playing. The tool returns `is_playing: false`. Just report that to the user.\n\n**`429 Too Many Requests`** = rate limit. Wait and retry once. If it keeps happening, you're looping — stop.\n\n**`401 Unauthorized` after a retry** — refresh token revoked. Tell the user to run `hermes auth spotify` again.\n\n## URI and ID formats\n\nSpotify uses three interchangeable ID formats. The tools accept all three and normalize:\n\n- URI: `spotify:track:0DiWol3AO6WpXZgp0goxAV` (preferred)\n- URL: `https://open.spotify.com/track/0DiWol3AO6WpXZgp0goxAV`\n- Bare ID: `0DiWol3AO6WpXZgp0goxAV`\n\nWhen in doubt, use full URIs. Search results return URIs in the `uri` field — pass those directly.\n\nEntity types: `track`, `album`, `artist`, `playlist`, `show`, `episode`. It belongs to the same category as Claude Code (Anthropic), Codex (OpenAI), and OpenClaw — autonomous coding and task-execution agents that use tool calling to interact with your system. Hermes works with any LLM provider (OpenRouter, Anthropic, OpenAI, DeepSeek, local models, and 15+ others) and runs on Linux, macOS, and WSL.\n\nWhat makes Hermes different:\n\n- **Self-improving through skills** — Hermes learns from experience by saving reusable procedures as skills. When it solves a complex problem, discovers a workflow, or gets corrected, it can persist that knowledge as a skill document that loads into future sessions. Skills accumulate over time, making the agent better at your specific tasks and environment.\n- **Persistent memory across sessions** — remembers who you are, your preferences, environment details, and lessons learned. Pluggable memory backends (built-in, Honcho, Mem0, and more) let you choose how memory works.\n- **Multi-platform gateway** — the same agent runs on Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal, Matrix, Email, and 10+ other platforms with full tool access, not just chat.\n- **Provider-agnostic** — swap models and providers mid-workflow without changing anything else. Credential pools rotate across multiple API keys automatically.\n- **Profiles** — run multiple independent Hermes instances with isolated configs, sessions, skills, and memory.\n- **Extensible** — plugins, MCP servers, custom tools, webhook triggers, cron scheduling, and the full Python ecosystem.\n\nPeople use Hermes for software development, research, system administration, data analysis, content creation, home automation, and anything else that benefits from an AI agent with persistent context and full system access.\n\n**This skill helps you work with Hermes Agent effectively** — setting it up, configuring features, spawning additional agent instances, troubleshooting issues, finding the right commands and settings, and understanding how the system works when you need to https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/main/scripts/install.sh | bash\n\n# Interactive chat (default)\nhermes\n\n# Single query\nhermes chat -q \"What is the capital of [command]\n\n  --version, -V             Show version\n  --resume, -r SESSION      Resume session by ID or title\n  --continue, -c [NAME]     Resume by name, or most recent session\n  --worktree, -w TEXT          Single query, non-interactive\n  -m, --model MODEL         Model (e.g. anthropic/claude-sonnet-4)\n  -t, --toolsets LIST       Comma-separated toolsets\n  --provider PROVIDER       Force doctor [--fix]       Check dependencies and config\nhermes status [--all]       Show component status\n``​`\n\n### Tools & Skills\n\n``​`\nhermes tools                Interactive tool enable/disable (curses UI)\nhermes tools list           Show all tools and status\nhermes tools enable NAME    Enable a toolset\nhermes tools disable NAME   Disable a toolset\n\nhermes skills list          List when frontmatter has no name)\nhermes skills inspect ID    Preview without installing\nhermes skills config        Enable/disable skills per platform\nhermes skills check         Check for ```text
Yes. I’d think about this as a **podcast intelligence pipeline**, not just “summarize podcasts.”

I saved a concrete plan here:

`.hermes/plans/2026-06-04_115024-podcast-intelligence-digest.md`

## Recommended approach

Start with a **manual prototype**, not a recurring automated system.

The feature should do four things:

1. **Discover episodes**
   - Pull from a curated list of podcast RSS feeds.
   - Later we can add Spotify, YouTube, Substack, etc.
   - RSS is the easiest/best v1 because it avoids app-specific auth.

2. **Rank what matters to you**
   - Score episodes based on your interests:
     - AI / agents / frontier labs
     - venture / markets / company building
     - MENA / geopolitics / defense
     - chips / compute / infrastructure
     - leadership / operating lessons
   - Use metadata first: title, guest, description, show, publish date.
   - Then use the LLM to decide: “Is this actually worth DJ’s time?”

3. **Summarize selected episodes**
   - For top candidates, get transcripts if available.
   - If no transcript exists, optionally download/transcribe audio.
   - Produce:
     - why this was selected
     - key takeaways
     - why it matters to you
     - possible action/follow-up
     - links to originals

4. **Create an audio digest**
   - Turn the selected summaries into an 8–15 minute digest podcast.
   - Use the improved audio lessons:
     - no reading labels aloud
     - high-quality TTS, ideally ElevenLabs
     - distinct voices only if we use a two-host format
     - polished, listenable script
   - Deliver MP3 to Telegram.

## My recommended V1

Do **not** start with “monitor every podcast everywhere.”

Start with:

- 10–20 curated podcast feeds
- last 7 days of episodes
- metadata-based ranking
- top 5 written digest
- top 3 deeper summaries
- one 8–10 minute audio digest
- manual run only

Then you calibrate:

> “These 2 were useful, these 3 were not, more of X, less of Y.”

After one or two calibration runs, we automate it.

## The architecture

### Job 1: Collector

Runs quietly.

- Fetch RSS feeds
- Store new episodes
- Deduplicate
- Save metadata
- No Telegram message unless it breaks

### Job 2: Digest producer

Runs only when approved.

- Reads recent episodes
- Scores relevance
- Picks best ones
- Gets transcripts/audio if needed
- Writes digest
- Generates MP3
- Sends to Telegram

## Key decision before building

We need your preferences before implementation:

1. **Source list**
   - Should I propose the first 20 podcasts?
   - Or do you want to give me your must-follow podcasts?

2. **Cadence**
   - Daily?
   - Weekdays?
   - 2–3x/week?
   - Weekly?

```text
Great — this is enough to define the v1 direction.

## Updated product shape

### Daily text digest

Every day:

- Look at new episodes from podcasts you actually listen to.
- Rank based on likely relevance to you.
- Transcribe only the finalists.
- Send a concise text digest with:
  - **Top episodes worth knowing about**
  - **Why it matters to DJ**
  - **Key takeaways**
  - **Whether to listen, skim, or skip**
  - **Original links**

This should be practical, not a generic podcast newsletter.

### Weekly audio digest

Once per week:

- 15–20 minute “podcast of podcasts”
- Two-host conversational format
- Structure:
  - “What’s interesting in the podcast world for DJ this week?”
  - Not episode-by-episode mechanically, but themes + best clips/ideas
  - Hosts should explain why the content matters, where there’s disagreement, and what DJ might do with it
- Generated from the week’s strongest podcast summaries
- Delivered as an MP3 to Telegram

## Source list strategy

We should build the curated list from **podcasts you actually listen to**, not from my generic recommendations.

Best path:

1. You give me your actual podcast list.
2. I map each show to an RSS feed.
3. We categorize each show:
   - must-monitor
   - maybe-monitor
   - low-priority
4. We do one manual digest run.
5. You calibrate what was useful.
6. Then we automate.

If you use Apple Podcasts / Spotify / Overcast / Pocket Casts, the easiest thing is probably:

- Send screenshots of your subscribed shows, or
- Export OPML if your app supports it, or
- Just list your top shows in text.

## Open-source model preference

For v1, I’d separate the model stack into three jobs:

### 1. Ranking / selection

Use a strong open-source reasoning model.

Good options:
- **Qwen3 / Qwen 2.5 72B**
- **DeepSeek R1 / V3**
- **Llama 3.3 70B**
- **Mistral Large / Mixtral-style models**, if available

This step needs judgment: “Is this worth DJ’s attention?”

### 2. Summarization

Use an open-source long-context model.

Good options:
- **Qwen long-context models**
- **Llama 3.1/3.3 70B**
- **DeepSeek V3**
- Possibly local if we have enough infra, but hosted open-source is likely better at first.

### 3. Audio script + production

Use the best available open-source-ish pipeline for the writing, then TTS separately.

Important distinction:

- **Open-source LLMs** can write the digest script.
- **High-quality TTS** is harder. Truly good open-source TTS exists, but operationally it can be heavier.
- For audio quality, we may still want ElevenLabs/OpenAI/MiniMax unless you specifically want open-source TTS even if quality/ops are worse.

If you want open-source TTS, candidates include:
- **Piper** — reliable but less premium
- **Coqui / XTTS** — better voice cloning potential, more setup
- **Bark / Parler-style models** — more expressive, less predictable
- **Dia / newer open audio models** if installable in this environment

My recommendation:
- Use open-source LLMs for ranking/summaries/scripts.
- Decide separately whether audio TTS must be open-source, because that affects quality a lot.

## Recommended v1 implementation

### Phase 1: Curated podcast inventory

You send me your actual podcast list.

I produce:

- `feeds.yaml`
- categories
- priority levels
- RSS URLs
- notes about which shows have transcripts / YouTube mirrors

### Phase 2: Manual daily digest prototype

One non-recurring run:

- Pull last 24–48 hours of episodes
- Rank by metadata
- Transcribe finalists only
- Produce text digest
- No audio yet

### Phase 3: Weekly audio prototype

After a few daily digests:

- Pick the best material from the week
- Generate 15–20 minute two-host script
- Produce audio
- You calibrate style, density, voice, pacing

### Phase 4: Automation

Only after approval:

- Daily text digest cron
- Weekly audio digest cron
- Both with cost/rate limits
- No recurring job enabled until you approve

## What I need next

Send me either:

1. **Your podcast app screenshots** showing subscribed/followed shows, or  
2. A typed list of the podcasts you actually listen to, or  
3. An OPML export if your podcast app supports it.

Best next move: send screenshots of your podcast subscriptions. I’ll turn that into the first curated feed list.
```

```text
That clarifies it a lot. The product is **not** “summarize my podcasts.” It is:

## A global podcast conversation radar

Find the conversations happening anywhere that are likely to matter to you professionally/intellectually — especially the ones you’d miss because you don’t subscribe to that show.

I updated the plan with this narrower-but-deeper definition:

`.hermes/plans/2026-06-04_115024-podcast-intelligence-digest.md`

## Your actual target profile

You want to surface conversations with:

- People who **move markets**
- People who **shape the business world**
- Leaders of major:
  - venture firms
  - PE firms
  - hedge funds
  - banks
  - asset managers
- CEOs/founders in influential industries
- AI leaders and frontier thinkers
- Intellectual framework-builders who change how you think

Examples of the “taste profile”:

- Gavin Baker
- Brian Chesky
- Paul Tudor Jones
- Stanley Druckenmiller
- Ben Horowitz
- Hemant Taneja / General Catalyst caliber
- Michael Pollan
- Nick Bostrom

And you want to know when relevant conversations happen on shows you **don’t** follow, like:

- All-In
- Marc & Ben Show
- Acquired
- similar business/AI/operator/investor shows

## What we should skip

For this product, I’d explicitly penalize:

- news recap podcasts
- political commentary
- defense unless directly tied to business/AI/markets
- energy/climate
- celebrity content
- generic VC fluff
- second-tier investor promo chatter
- mainstream reactive takes
- biographical origin-story episodes without ideas

That matters because otherwise “business podcast digest” quickly becomes garbage.

## The ranking test

The episode should only surface if it’s likely to give you one of:

- an action you want to take
- a changed mental model
- a new framework
- a professional/personal toolkit addition

That should become the core scoring rule.

## Proposed discovery architecture

I’d use a **three-ring source model**.

### Ring 1: Your serious subscriptions

These calibrate your taste and provide baseline coverage:

- Bloomberg Surveillance
- Conversations with Tyler
- Dwarkesh
- Invest Like the Best
- Lenny’s Podcast
- Odd Lots
- Plain English
- RenMac
- Sharp Tech
- Stratechery
- Ezra Klein
- The Gray Area
- Knowledge Project
- Tim Ferriss
- etc.

### Ring 2: High-signal shows you may not follow

Curated business / AI / operator / investor shows:

- All-In
- Marc & Ben
- Acquired
- a16z shows
- top founder/operator interview shows
- AI business/research podcasts
- investor/allocator podcasts

These get monitored because maybe **1 in 5** matters, but you don’t want to miss that one.

### Ring 3: Guest/event discovery

This is where we “scrape the world.”

We search podcast indexes and web sources for:

- major investors
- major CEOs/founders
- AI lab leaders
- market-moving allocators
- intellectual framework people

So if Ben Horowitz, Gavin Baker, Brian Chesky, Bostrom, etc. appears on some podcast you don’t follow, it gets caught.

## Scoring rubric

Every episode gets scored on:

1. **Guest caliber**
   - Does this person matter?

2. **Topic relevance**
   - AI, markets, capital allocation, company building, strategy, business environment.

3. **Conversation depth**
   - Real conversation or promo fluff?

4. **Novelty**
   - New framework? Contrarian insight? Rare access?

5. **Actionability**
   - Could this change what you do or how you think?

6. **Anti-fluff penalty**
   - Celebrity, politics, news recap, generic VC, shallow promo.

Labels:

- **Must listen**
- **Digest only**
- **Skim if interested**
- **Skip**

## Cadence

### Daily text

Every day:

- What came out today / last 24h
- What matters
- Why it matters to DJ
- Listen / skim / skip
- Transcribe only finalists

### Weekly audio

Once per week:

- 15–20 minute two-host conversational digest
- “What’s interesting in the podcast world for DJ this week?”
- Synthesis by theme, not just episode list
- Audio generated from the week’s best material

## Open-source model direction

For the intelligence work, we can use open-source/open-weight models:

- Qwen
- DeepSeek
- Llama
- Mistral open models

Use them for:

- ranking
- transcript summaries
- weekly synthesis
- audio script writing

TTS is separate. Open-source TTS may be lower quality or heavier operationally. I’d evaluate that separately rather than quietly giving you bad voices.

## Recommended next step

Before building the jobs, we should define the **source universe**.

I should produce a draft with:

1. **Ring 1** — your known serious subscriptions  
2. **Ring 2** — 30–50 high-signal business/AI/investor/operator shows  
3. **Guest watchlist** — 100–200 people grouped by:
   - investors
   - CEOs/founders
   - AI leaders
   - allocators/macro people
   - intellectual framework thinkers
4. **Skip taxonomy** — what to filter out

Then you edit/approve that. After that, we do one manual digest run before automating anything.

- a new framework

- news recaps

The core should not be “episodes from shows DJ follows.”

- feeds/shows, and
- people/guest appearances.
